The Atlantic
A Deeply Layered Novel About Masculinity
2026-08-11

A Deeply Layered Novel About Masculinity

The Atlantic | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Tangled themes of violence and masculinity run like buried cables through Chang-rae Lee’s work. One of contemporary American literature’s major chroniclers of manhood, he’s been writing for three decades about assimilation, suburban alienation, and the mystery of how his characters survive loneliness and, often, shocking loss. Lee uses his careful prose to sink slowly and deeply into the minds of men who resist thinking about their feelings, even as they ruminate obsessively about their past griefs. His new novel, A Tender Age, would belong in this meditative lineage, except that its narrator, a middle-aged man named Jeon-Gi, doesn’t resist emotion in the slightest. He’s as sensitive to—and as fascinated by—its lingering effects as his creator is. And unlike some of Lee’s earlier protagonists, this character wants to know himself. Jeon-Gi opens the book by confessing that he’s consumed by the “nature of certain youthful happenings, how they can become a knot in the flesh that every other occurrence must bypass in order for you to know yourself.” He then recounts, in vivid detail, the events of the summer he turned 11, which have haunted him ever since. The novel wobbles between manhood and boyhood, clarity and confusion, self-knowledge and a windmilling search for identity by a 10-year-old faced with challenges he’s too young to comprehend. Nearly all of Lee’s fiction—from his debut, Native Speaker (1995), through his war novels, A Gesture Life (1999) and The Surrendered (2010), to his rollicking bildungsroman, My Year Abroad (2021)—shares a worry that the masculine ideals of self-sufficiency, strength, and swagger can lead to brutality. Now, for the first time, Lee traces the concern back to boyhood, a risky move: Young Jeon-Gi’s travails are conventional ones, the fodder of many coming-of-age stories. He gets bullied, lands in trouble defending himself, and then attempts a reinvention. What sets A Tender Age apart is its layered narration, in which a reflective adult and resurrected child together lead the reader onward as Lee strings threats of violence through the text, building thrillerlike tension. As the eldest child of Korean immigrants, Jeon-Gi feels called on to be “the head of the column” as he, his parents, and his sister navigate life in the United States. Among peers, meanwhile, he feels rising pressure to prove that he’s a cool insider, not a weakling or a “wuss,” to use a word Lee repeats often in the book’s opening section. A lot is at stake in this effort, which abruptly turns Jeon-Gi from a good boy into a troubled youth—a juvenile-justice case, in fact. Lee tracks the adult Jeon-Gi’s fascination with this time in his life, which tested his maturity and masculinity in ways that were both traumatic and exhilarating. In the process, as the novel charts Jeon-Gi’s path toward becoming an American man, Lee dares to linger over the allure of aggression and dominance, probing rather than skirting or accepting it. Like any coming-of-age story, A Tender Age begins before the Fall. Jeon-Gi, lucky boy, has two Edens: his nuclear family and a “tourniquet tight” gaggle of friends in his neighborhood, a down-at-the-heels 1970s New York suburb where “the adults, for whatever reason, whether they were working a job or not, left us on our own, and to one another.” Jeon-Gi’s mother is an exception. She is the attentive hub of their strikingly close family, and he idolizes her. “I loved my father and my sister to death,” he recalls, “but it was my mother who spun and wove the fabric of my existence.” As her eldest—and as a son—he’s the focus of her doting, which extends to bringing him treats on the otherwise parent-free playground. Early in the novel, a disturbingly clever bully named Katie Croker targets Jeon-Gi, damaging in one cruel move both his childish dignity and his belief in his mother’s omnipotence. She coerces Jeon-Gi into cursing at his mother, a scene full of the emotional crosscurrents that destabilize and deepen what might seem like a slight book. Deriding his mother’s limited English while clearly envying her warm presence, Katie deploys a well-targeted dig: “You think everybody gets that?” Katie demands of the snack deliveries. “You think it’s normal?” Read: The novelist who learned to write anger—and it’s aftermath Jeon-Gi does, in a sense, assume that the treatment he gets is normal, aware though he is that even his sister, Ella, doesn’t receive it. Looking back decades later, he sees that Ella “learned early on to make the best of whatever she got, to always just move on.” Meanwhile, he “expected things to come to me easily, and when they didn’t, I readily gave myself over to the muck of my frustrated thoughts, allowing the foulness to seep in wherever it had a path and then fill my gut with bile.” After the Katie incident, Jeon-Gi starts to swing between eagerness to delight his parents and impress his friends and a new readiness to indulge a more anti-social side of himself. He’s been initiated into the reality that his playground world, for all of its camaraderie, also harbors dog-eat-dog energy. He starts scamming a boy who’s bad enough at math to need help calculating change for the ice-cream truck: “I liked Paul and Paul liked me,” he recalls, “but because I loved ice cream and because I could, I skimmed him most every time.” “Because I could” is, of course, highly compelling logic when you’re 10; still, Jeon-Gi’s older self sees something more than ordinary childhood behavior in his new urge to exploit. Jeon-Gi does have good reason for this budding desire for power, as aggression escalates in his unsupervised realm. A former friend, Joshua, begins calling him racist names, and a troubled older boy tails and sadistically harasses him. Fresh from his Katie ordeal, Jeon-Gi decides not to seek parental or peer help. Instead, he devises a private plan that involves bringing his mother’s bread knife to school and that makes him lonelier than he’s ever been in his ten years of life. Jeon-Gi’s decision to retaliate awakens him to his own capacity and—more important—his own desire to imagine behaving violently. While smuggling the knife into his book bag, he admires the “steely, dead-faced boy” he sees in the mirror and debates whether he wants his truly scary tormentor “scarred for life or simply dead.” Naturally, nothing goes quite as he anticipated, and Jeon-Gi, child that he is, loses control in a conflict with Joshua. In this moment, unlike anywhere else in the text, Lee allows the grown-up Jeon-Gi to dodge his memories. He relates the scene as if he’s blacked it out: “They say I screamed, in Korean and English. You wanna die? Ju-gu-leh? You wanna die right now?” But the lines that come next show that Jeon-Gi has not erased all of the moment. He admits that he can still feel the knife sometimes, and adds, referring to Joshua, “To this day I’ve never seen a person so scared.” For the rest of the novel, the young Jeon-Gi is consumed by the question of whether he wants to be scary. As he travels through the justice system, the professional verdict is no, he doesn’t. He gets sent to therapy rather than detention, in part because in the world of juvenile courts, as the adult Jeon-Gi recognizes, he’s “a rare creature, akin to some tiny tree primate who foraged only at night, this clean-cut, soft-spoken, somewhat overcourteous Oriental kid who came from an intact and evidently loving, supportive family.” As a child, he hardly grasps the reprieve his demographic rarity offers him. He’s too immersed in the mystery of whether he’s a gentle child or a “dead-faced boy.” Although drawn at times to the latter, he recoils from envisioning his parents burdened with the stigma of a deviant child. As he searches himself for signs of inherent violence, the tenor of the narration wavers between fear and hope, familiar emotional poles in Lee’s work. Read: A novel that captures the allure of the scam The grown Jeon-Gi wavers too. He can hardly bear to remember brandishing the bread knife, yet can’t let go of the thought that he really could have been a violent boy. Lee captures his enthrallment in the novel’s most gripping, surreally disorienting scene—a tour de force in which Jeon-Gi gets sent to visit a prison alongside a group of teenage offenders deemed in need of being scared straight. He clearly doesn’t belong there, and the narrative shifts between wannabe tough guy and nervous child with almost every new paragraph. Even as his adult self recalls hoping for a “tour-ending treat of an ice cream sandwich,” he also remembers feeling thrilled at the “breathtaking” power wielded by a frighteningly charismatic young inmate. By the day’s end, the young Jeon-Gi is newly confident that he can “step outside of a moment in order to commit some transgressive act”—a capacity that most if not all people have, but that he wants to see as a sign of his toughness. When he soon heads to Korean church camp, he is sure that he’s effectively an adult, or at least “someone not easily besieged by emotions.” Of course, Jeon-Gi is still a child, though in fact his two-week-camp agenda is a mature one in its way: He sets off having decided that he will demonstrate that he’s a good son, not a cause for adult concern. But once he arrives, A Tender Age takes on a threatening tone. Lee weaves in warnings that something awful is coming, and hints that Jeon-Gi will cause it, which freights the boy’s ordinary transgressions—lying about his home life, cheating at games—with suspense. The prose slows down, taking on a luxuriance that makes each scene ominous. When Jeon-Gi and his bunk mates hike to a waterfall, they stand below it in a “natural quarry, its walls composed of a smoother, darker rock almost completely covered in moss.” Beautiful though this “amphitheater of a deep, earthy green that surrounded various pools of water” is, it seems the perfect backdrop for a dangerous spectacle. Yet A Tender Age’s oscillation between light and dark never lets up. Its foreboding camp scenes are shot through with understated humor and a lingering sense of innocence. Take Jeon-Gi’s infatuation with an independent, enigmatic girl named Susan, which is strikingly pure. The older Jeon-Gi recalls that sex “was as distant from me as the idea of going to a job each day like my father did. I understood it was coming for me but had zero desire.” His crush is all about watchful worship, a fascination with nonconformist autonomy; so is Jeon-Gi’s idolization of his thoughtful counselor, Ben Frost, a religious hippie whom Lee always calls by his full name, as if to remind readers of how snowily white he is. Counselors aside, the camp is an all–Korean American idyll where for the first time, Jeon-Gi can imagine himself as a hero “at the center of the throng, celebrated by raw throats and hearts.” Still, vengeance and violence reappear when a smooth bully named Dickens becomes his nemesis. Jeon-Gi devises a plan to leave the older boy “marked forever with something like a limp, such that every step he took he’d have to think about what he’d done.” Ultimately, the camper who goes home “marked forever” is not Dickens but Jeon-Gi. The novel’s climax—about which I will say only that the novel’s escalating tension pays off—bequeaths him the permanent “knot in the flesh” he mentions at the book’s start. In an effort to loosen it, he moves as far as he can from “the old places,” yet, as the novel attests, he remains consumed by this childhood interlude. The identity of the dead-faced boy he saw in the mirror evidently remains the conundrum of his life. A Tender Age ends without answers, though Lee grants Jeon-Gi one closing moment of clarity, a move he has made in many of his novels. In this case, it’s a memory that is less a knot than a touchstone. He describes a Sunday when, as a “striving, struggling young man,” he broke his rule of avoiding the sites of his childhood and walked by the church that had sent him to camp. As he drew near, “scores of well-dressed congregants and their children were coming out from the nave and down the main steps, like so many fish streaming down the chute of a river.” Church and rushing water—that fraught summer comes to mind, but the image is centrally one of community and connection, of family, of a life of forward momentum. Jeon-Gi can’t join or understand the “swaying, merry-sounding talk” around him: His old congregation has switched languages, from Korean to Chinese. Still, he approaches with great hope. “It could have been wonderful,” he recalls. “I waded in.” ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Spain Is Ready for an Extraordinary Run of Eclipses
2026-08-11

Spain Is Ready for an Extraordinary Run of Eclipses

The Atlantic | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Normal days in Spain can suddenly turn otherworldly. On a trip to Málaga in April, for instance, during Semana Santa, my friends and I happened upon a midnight procession of about 50 men in hoods who were carrying a 1,000-pound sculpture of a crucified Jesus Christ on their shoulders, lit by hundreds of candles that swayed with every step they took. But starting tomorrow, Spain’s regular drumbeat of fairs, street parties, and patron-saint festivals will be accompanied by improbable astrological luck. After a century of no total solar eclipses over the Iberian Peninsula, and none in mainland Europe in the 21st century so far, three solar eclipses will be observable from Spain in the next 500 days or so. This is “very, very unusual,” Sandra Benitez Herrera, the head of science communication and education at the European Space Agency, told the German broadcaster DW last month. It will also probably be a lot of fun. The Spanish government expects hundreds of thousands of astrotourists—including me—for Eclipse Number One this week. In most places on the path of totality, the sun will be fully eclipsed for about a minute or two, which makes this the shortest of the three celestial events. But totality will hit at sunset, a coincidence that amplifies the experience of the eclipse and occurs only about once in a decade globally, Kelsey Johnson, an astrophysicist and the former president of the American Astronomical Society, told me. The shadows will feel strange, she said: Sunset already makes shadows long, and the eclipse will add its own distortions, including the white squiggles of light that sometimes coat the ground just before totality. Then, after the sun has set, people can gawk at the 60 to 100 meteors that are expected an hour, as the Perseid meteor shower peaks against the blank slate of a new moon. Madrid and Barcelona will see 99 percent totality, but, as Annie Dillard wrote in her classic 1982 essay, “seeing a partial eclipse bears the same relation to seeing a total eclipse as kissing a man does to marrying him.” So eclipse chasers are traveling to smaller towns that will go completely dark, where visitors are liable to encounter the particularities of Spanish August. This is the month when Spaniards return from cities to their hometowns, and the heat makes people nocturnal. They throw summer festivals, put on concerts, and release the bulls. The eclipse only adds to a general sense that this is a time to cast routine aside. Take A Coruña, the first major city in Spain that will experience totality. The city’s minute of total darkness will end at 8:29 p.m.; half an hour later, A Coruña’s professional football team, Deportivo, plays Real Madrid for the first time in nearly a decade after clawing its way back from a lower-tier league. “A bunch of people are going to be looking at the eclipse right outside the stadium and then going straight to the game,” Pablo Gomez-Garcia, a University of Virginia law student who returns to A Coruña every August to see family, told me. Others might chase the eclipse with a concert: The city is in the midst of a monthslong festival that is named for María Pita, a woman who stabbed an English captain with his own spear after her husband was mortally wounded defending the city, and that features daily outdoor performances in the evenings. The performer tomorrow will be an orchestra called the Satellites. The city is expecting so many visitors—20,000 are supposed to enter by cruise ship alone today and tomorrow—that its government decided to close one popular viewpoint, a Roman-era lighthouse on the northwest coast, to avoid trampling. In Briviesca, a town in the north of Spain, the eclipse overlaps with the weeklong patron-saint festival. On the day of the eclipse, the city will host, among many free activities, a fishing contest, performances by local bands, a parade of “giants and big heads”—a tradition from the Middle Ages that involves people donning 13-foot papier-mâché costumes depicting royals and monsters—and a toro de fuego, a metal bull filled with fireworks that will shoot sparks at people in the town square. In Tarragona, where I’m viewing the eclipse, there will also be a showcase of human-tower making, during which people stack themselves into six-to-10-person-tall monuments, a Catalan tradition. These local practices are meant to be outlandish visual spectacles that eject you from the everyday. The weirdness of witnessing an eclipse can conjure a similar sense of awe and delight—and the eclipse fervor that has gripped many visitors suggests a hunger for that sense. The colors change. The shadows get very sharp. “You sound like a crazy person, but the feeling is like, I must not be on Earth anymore,” Jesse Tomlinson, an eclipse chaser and the creator of a popular app that helps people choose eclipse viewpoints, told me. The wind direction shifts, and the temperature suddenly drops. You grow giddy to see another. Johnson, the astrophysicist, said that her flight from Virginia to Barcelona was full of eclipse chasers chatting across rows. Like any massive party, these eclipse celebrations have the potential to get out of hand. The population of Mallorca, for instance, already swells in August, and eclipse tourists are heaping onto the island’s temporary population. The regional government of the Balearic Islands has decided to not promote the eclipse to avoid creating an even larger surge in visitors, and is papering the area with reminders to avoid engaging in dangerous observation practices, going drunk boating, and lighting fires. On the podcast Majorca Mallorca, Nick Brown, a Brit living in Mallorca, predicted that “this is going to be the colapso to end all colapsos.” In the days leading up to the eclipse, local Spanish governments have denied permits to eclipse festivals marketed to Anglophone ravers. But for the most part, Spain’s central government has gone all-in on promoting this eclipse and has called the Iberian Peninsula “the only populated place in the world where the total solar eclipse of August 12 can be fully enjoyed.” (This is a bit of a snub to Iceland’s capital of Reykjavík, where totality is more likely to be marred by bad weather.) The setting you experience an eclipse in does matter, Johnson said. She is leading a tour group off the coast of Mallorca from a boat that seats 80; natural settings, she said, can help establish the transcendent connection to Earth you want to find during the brief window where the moon blocks the sun. Still, anyone in the path of totality has a chance to experience something out of the ordinary, even if there are no human towers for miles around. And this eclipse is really just a rehearsal for the one to come. Spain’s next eclipse, in August 2027, is already being billed as “the Eclipse of the Century” for the six minutes of totality it will bring.
The Atlantic
Are Men to Blame for the Birth-Rate Collapse?
2026-08-11

Are Men to Blame for the Birth-Rate Collapse?

The Atlantic | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Society is running out of babies, and no one can agree on why. Most people around the world, including Americans, live in a country where overall fertility is below what demographers call the “replacement rate,” meaning that without migration, the population will shrink from one generation to the next. In some places, the birth rate is so low that each generation is set to be half the size of the previous one. A number of theories have been put forward to explain why this is happening. Some experts point to smartphones and social media, others to the cost of child care, still others to existential dread or overparenting. Claudia Goldin, the Nobel Prize–winning labor economist, favors another explanation: It’s the men. In a widely discussed new working paper, she argues that husbands still fail to do their fair share of housework and child care, forcing their wives to choose between procreation and career success. As a result, women are making the economically rational decision to have fewer kids. “The more that men can credibly signal they will be dependable ‘dads’ and not disappointing ‘duds,’ the higher the birth rate will be in the face of greater female agency,” Goldin writes in a companion essay. Goldin’s theory is attractive because it promises to resolve an uncomfortable tension in the fertility discussion. Historically, falling birth rates have accompanied increases in women’s education and autonomy. This was widely seen as a good thing when it meant going from seven kids on average to two or three. Now that birth rates have fallen below replacement, however, calls to reverse the decline can sound like attacks on feminism itself. But if Goldin is right, there’s no tension. Under her framework, rising equality for women helped bring birth rates down, and only more equality will get them back up. Unfortunately, the theory does not appear to be true. The data simply do not support it. For decades, men have been increasing their share of housework and child care. But the number of babies just keeps falling. When a country gets richer, its fertility tends to decline. But in the 1990s, scholars noticed that some high-income countries, such as France and Norway, had much higher fertility rates than others, such as Japan and Italy. What could explain this variation? Some hypothesized that traditions change more slowly than economies. In the Japans and Italys, they theorized, women were being told to be modern outside the home while still being asked to perform most of the labor inside it, leaving them burned-out and uninterested in having the usual two or three children. In the Frances and Norways, meanwhile, people were having more children because they were confident that things would be fairer in their households. “Women are able to see what happens to other women,” Peter McDonald, an Australian demographer who, in 2000, wrote one of the most famous papers proposing this theory, told me. Goldin’s new paper builds off the work of McDonald and others. Her theory is that countries with low fertility are experiencing a “mismatch” between women’s desire to be modern and men’s desire for them not to be. The paper proposes a simplified economic model to describe reproductive decisions. In the model, men are either “dads” who do sufficient child care and housework or “duds” who do not. If an educated woman marries a “dud” and has children, he won’t help out much, forcing her to limit her career and end up earning the same wage as if she had not gone to college. If she instead marries a “dad,” she can have kids and still realize her full economic potential. At a societal level, because these women don’t know which type of man they’ve married until after they have had a child, they’ll base their decision on whether to have children in proportion to the ratio of dads to duds in their country. The fewer the duds, the more comfortable they will be giving birth to the next generation without fear of a wage penalty. Marc Novicoff: The birth-rate crisis isn’t as bad as you’ve heard—it’s worse All economic models are simplifications, but some make more reasonable assumptions than others. Goldin’s model assumes that only college-educated women weigh whether to have children, but in some countries, including the United States, women who haven’t graduated from college are shifting toward childlessness faster than women who have. Another key assumption is that dad-or-dud status is randomly and blindly assigned. In fact, prospective couples are probably sorting more finely than ever. People are more likely to marry other people of similar education levels, the share of marriages that end in divorce is down, and the time spent living together before having children is up. We have never had more time to examine our partners before having children, and the chance of randomly drawing someone with sharply different beliefs about household gender roles is small and shrinking. An even bigger problem with the theory is the data ostensibly backing it up. As a proxy for the share of duds in a given country, Goldin uses survey data on how men and women spend their time in a set of OECD countries. In places where women report doing a lot more unpaid work (such as child care and household chores) than men, Goldin shows, the total fertility rate is lower. In places where the work is divvied up more evenly, fertility is higher. Her paper includes a graph that shows a strong correlation between the two factors. The trouble is that the relationship that the graph describes holds up only when you stick to Goldin’s somewhat idiosyncratic selection of data points. The fertility rates she uses for her y-axis, for example, are all from 2019, regardless of when the time-use survey was completed in a given country, and despite the fact that birth rates in many countries have fallen sharply since then. This creates distortions in various directions: Finland, for example, completed the survey she uses in 2010, when fertility stood at about 1.9 children per woman, but Goldin plots it at its 2019 fertility rate of 1.35. That happens to be close to Finland’s current rate, but the same isn’t true of Estonia, where fertility fell from about 1.7 in 2019 to 1.2 in 2025. Goldin restricts her sample to OECD countries with time-use surveys completed from 2009 to 2019, but leaves out two of them—Mexico and Turkey—in which women did a large share of the unpaid work but fertility was nonetheless high in 2019. She then adds Portugal and Denmark, whose time-use surveys were conducted in 1999 and 2001, and leaves out six other countries—Australia, Austria, Lithuania, Slovenia, Latvia, and Ireland—that conducted their surveys sometime from 1999 to 2009 and whose fertility rates mostly don’t fit the pattern of the ones included. In some cases, newer time-use data exist. Factoring in the latest surveys from Poland and the U.S., for example, would show a roughly 25-percent-smaller gap in unpaid work. If you include the countries that Goldin omits, and use the most recent time-use and fertility data, what you are left with is virtually no relationship at all. The slope flattens, and the amount of variation in fertility rates that is explained by the time-use differences (known in statistics as “the R-squared”) approaches 2 percent. When I spoke with Goldin about the paper, she was open to critiques about it. “All the comments that you’ve raised, I would raise about my own work,” she told me. She granted that “you can change the model in a hundred different ways.” As for the data points, she said she was trying to stay consistent with a previous version of the paper. “Sometimes, when you’re working on something, you begin with using one set of data and you build off that. And then you don’t ask, Well, maybe I should use something else.” Later, over email, she said that the graph was “not a major part of my paper or my research” and that “cross-country analysis is to be avoided.” Derek Thompson: When a promotion leads to divorce Versions of Goldin’s argument made by other academics have also been hampered by methodological issues. In a Slate article published last year, Darby Saxbe, a psychologist at the University of Southern California, argued that “dads can fix the birth rate” by doing more chores and child care. Her piece featured its own chart comparing men’s share of unpaid work to fertility rates in OECD countries. When I tried to re-create the chart, the results looked totally different. Over email, we figured out the likely culprit: The data for Saxbe’s chart, she explained, were sourced by AI, which seems to have hallucinated some of the numbers. Goldin told me that the original idea for her paper came from an influential 2008 study titled “Will the Stork Return to Europe and Japan? Understanding Fertility Within Developed Nations.” The authors of that paper showed a strong relationship between what young mothers said their male partners contributed to care and housework in a given country and that country’s fertility rate. According to the authors’ math, a 10-percentage-point increase in men’s unpaid-labor share was associated with 0.4 more births per woman—an enormous difference. So the correlation between fertility and men’s contributions at home was robust at one point. But, if you update the data, it disappears. The authors of “Will the Stork Return” used surveys and fertility rates from 2002. Using the 2022 version of the survey (the most recent available) and 2024 fertility rates (also the most recent available for most countries) produces an almost perfectly flat relationship, one in which variation in men’s share of housework explains essentially none of the variation in a country’s fertility. Birth rates still differ among rich countries, but the male share of housework apparently has nothing to do with it. “Will the Stork Return” included optimistic predictions for the future. Observing that “men in all high-income countries appear to be taking on a larger share of household duties,” the researchers forecast that “high-income countries with the lowest fertility rates are likely to see an increase in fertility in the coming decades.” That has not come true. Men’s share of housework has continued to rise, but fertility has only declined further. This set of facts applies to basically every country for which data exist. In Finland, the gap in unpaid work roughly halved from 2010 to 2021; the fertility rate went from from 1.9 to 1.5, and now sits at 1.3. In South Korea, the gap in household work shrank 42 percent from 1999 to 2024; over that time, South Korean fertility rates went from 1.4 births per woman to 0.75. “I would guess that the theory is right and there’s other things going on that have completely overwhelmed the theory,” Bruce Sacerdote, one of the economists who wrote the 2008 paper, told me. But if men’s increased participation in unpaid labor is pushing fertility up, the effect is undetectable. If men helping out more at home could reverse the birth-rate decline, that would be great news. It would ease the burden on women and simultaneously help save the world from aging populations, stagnant economies, and crumbling pension systems. Alas, the experiment has been run, and the results are in: Men are chipping in more, and the number of babies is still falling. That doesn’t mean they couldn’t do more, but it does mean that doing more is unlikely to fix the situation. And telling women that they’re surrounded by “duds” seems unlikelier still.
The Atlantic
Disunion
2026-08-11

Disunion

The Atlantic | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
I took longer than some people to start calling it the war. And by some people, I mainly mean my husband, Asher. “This is it, Natalie,” he said to me that first Friday, back in June 2036, after watching the news coverage from Oklahoma City for three hours straight. siege: more than 300 killed and 400 wounded in terrorist military attacks, the headlines read. “It’s started. The civil war.” He claims I rolled my eyes. I don’t think I did. “Well,” I said, “these idiots in Oklahoma obviously want us to think that’s what it is.” Ever since I met Ash, in 2021, he has both dreaded and expected this or that political nightmare just around the corner, some other ghastly shoe always about to drop. Occasionally, one did. To me, his sincere alarm often seemed mixed with a doomer’s touch of anticipatory relish. And he always thought I was too disengaged from politics, sometimes guilty of “willful obliviousness” and “toxic positivity,” generally “in denial” about the inevitable American apocalypse, the end, the horror, the horror. Even now, seven years after the insurgency ended, he says I’m too quick to move on. “You know people are still getting killed, right?” he asked during a call last November, on our first Thanksgiving apart in 23 years. I replied that people are also still dying from Nipah and COVID but that not even most of his fellow San Franciscans wear a mask. During that summer of the first attacks, I referred to the violence a few times as the hillbilly intifada. I meant it ironically, disparagingly, but Ash didn’t approve of comparing the Plains Patriot Militia to Palestinian terrorists. I referred more earnestly to the early bombings and shootings as the Troubles. It seemed right to me, because I thought the militia attacks might turn out to be like the decades of organized violence in Northern Ireland, continuing sporadically for years and years. Ash objected to that one too. “You like it,” he said, “because it doesn’t take a side. And you’re a twee Anglophile even when it comes to these gangs of mass-murdering fascists right here, right now.” “Keep calm and carry on,” I replied. “Although in this instance? Actually more Hibernophile?” Writing down and reading this touché of mine now, I am reminded how comments like that can be annoying in a marriage, as opposed to a movie or a show or a novel. Calling the mass shootings and bombings and assassinations and kidnappings and disappearances a rebellion or an insurgency or an uprising, Ash said when they started, unless modified by Christian-fascist or white-nationalist or neo-Nazi, “just romanticizes these psychos and their ‘anti-tyranny’ delusions.” In another conversation that first summer, he referred to them as “your psycho friends,” which prompted a big fight. “My friends” because I grew up in Tennessee. It was a war, and wars are ghastly. Things fell apart. Anarchy and a blood-dimmed tide were loosed upon our world for a while. Twenty-eight thousand Americans were killed, half of them innocent civilians, and another 45,000 wounded. But it was a brief, manageable war as wars go, 18 months long. Altogether not much compared with longer and/or ghastlier wars, like the U.S. versus Iraq or Ukraine versus Russia or Israel versus Palestine or Iran versus Israel. I couldn’t bear the people who immediately, eagerly started referring to it as Civil War II––or, worse, Civil War 2, as if it were a game. If what we’d put ourselves through had really been equivalent to what our American great-great-great-great-great-grandparents put themselves through in the 1860s, a hundred times as many of us would’ve fought and maybe 10 million of us would’ve died. On the other hand, Ash said to me a year ago: “So you’re happy to call the big one that didn’t succeed a civil war, but not this smaller one that did?” Fair enough, professor. I finally ended my semantic resistance. Okay, I told Ash, if history repeated and this counts as our second civil war, the first one was tragedy and this one is farce plus tragedy. But my favorite remains the term Walt Whitman used for the first one, the War of Secession. God knows the past decade has been painful. Crushingly. It’s a lot. It’s the most. And I expect the sadness to linger for the rest of my life. But we don’t all now inhabit some Mad Max or The Blazes or The Last of Us hellscape, do we? To quote a catchphrase of my father’s, who lived through the Cuban Revolution: “Podría ser peor,” and occasionally “Pudo haber sido mucho peor.” Could be worse. Could’ve been lots worse. So annoying to me when I was a kid unhappy about something, yet so often seems so true now. I’m a writer, fussy about words. I’ve always thought way too many things are “tragedies” in America, mainly as a result of media hacks defaulting to that cliché every time they possibly can—robo-empathy, I called it before AI newscasters became a real thing. Life is half tragedy, for Christ’s sake, maybe more than half, which is one of the reasons I grade almost everything on a curve. And don’t we all, really? If we didn’t, we’d come undone contemplating the numberless tragedies, one occurring every instant in some life somewhere. The violence was horrible, a nonstop psycho-stravaganza filled with daily terrors and tragedies that made me cry often. I still think of the random towns such as Inkster, Michigan, and Chuichu, Arizona, that nobody had ever heard of until their name became an overnight synonym for mass death or another horror. Until a week or two afterward, when some unfortunate new nowhere became suddenly infamous. Come to think of it, that happened 180 years ago, when little towns such as Chickamauga and Spotsylvania Courthouse got famous overnight. For decades after that civil war, Americans were familiar with those battlefield names. But have you heard of them, let alone do you know where they are? (Not far from where I grew up and not far from where I went to college, respectively.) You’re reading this, so you probably do know what happened in Hiroshima and Nagasaki a hundred Augusts ago, but the other day, I saw a poll that found that more than half of Americans have no idea. My point is that almost everything’s got a half-life, and surely you sense the half-life of our own recent trauma. Yes, cutting up the country still feels weird, and probably always will. But it’s 2045, and the acute, constant fear is gone. The pain is fading. Easy for me to say; the violence barely touched me. Nobody I loved was killed. That’s not counting a very close friend of Asher’s. Or two of my childhood friends and a cousin I met once. And the ex-husband of a college friend murdered in federal prison while serving time for the Fort Lewis attack of 2037, the cockamamie military-prison-breakout scheme in the state of Washington that involved a homemade 10-man submarine painted to resemble a penis. But beyond the individual tragedies endured by individual Americans, I sincerely wonder: In the fairly near future, how great of a tragedy will most people consider the whole thing? In 25 or 30 years, when our children are our age, will it seem to them like such a big deal? Or to their hypothetical children? I kind of doubt it. Given the way things are playing out. An improbable plan for “Disunion” was improbably agreed to, then supported by a large majority of us—large majorities, I should say, on both sides—and negotiated and renegotiated over the past seven years. Messily, fitfully, ruefully, exhaustively, meticulously, more peacefully than not. And now very nearly complete. When he was president, Andrew Im loved saying “win-win” and “win-win-win” and “everyone wins” about almost everything, including Disunion. In my opinion, everyone lost. But everyone who calls themselves American, from sea to sea and border to border, is still using the same air-traffic-control and interstate-highway systems, eating the same Mega Macs and GimmeChimichangas, sharing intimacies with the same digital phantoms, and about to spend a Sunday all watching Super Bowl LXXVII. Kumbaya. And the U.S. stock market’s now back up to where it was before the war, higher, even. Plus ça change. The people who negotiated the details agreed long ago, for tedious reasons involving fiscal years, that Disunion Day would fall on the 30th of June. Which turned out to mean that last year’s Independence Day weekend, from Thursday the 30th to Monday the Fourth of July, was also the beginning of the new era, the United States of America and the Free American Republic independent of each other. (I’m more of a Christian than not, Catholic, and I was pleased that, in the end, the FAR didn’t go with any of the Christian or Christian-y names under consideration, such as American Dominion and the Christian Republic of America. Apparently what eliminated the latter was its similarity to the Islamic Republics of Iran, Pakistan, and Mauritania.) Such a perfectly strange, sad, can’t-make-it-up punctuation to our last couple of decades, our last 269 years. Asher’s Aunt Lizzie, whom I’d love to be at 85, says Disunion weekend struck her as “a trippy minor-key reboot of the bicentennial,” the Fourth of July in 1976, on which, she told us, she lost her virginity at Disneyland as the fireworks went off, backstage at a concert by a fictional-but-real band called the Monkees. “Of course,” she added, “the fireworks go off every night in Disneyland and Disney World, don’t they?” Back in the 2010s and early ’20s, when I was in school, I thought that if some people could slake some of their fear and anger and unhappiness and confusion by rage-baiting and name-calling online, or getting off on Trump’s live performances, fine—go crazy. I thought it was like how porn and violent games are safety valves that keep some unhappy men from becoming rapists and killers. I figured that one point of a culture war was to avoid having an actual shooting war. In the early ’20s at Brown, where I got my MFA and Ash had his first teaching job, I said too publicly, after protesters splashed paint on an Israeli novelist giving a talk, that I was “grateful our activists are only paintball Red Guards,” that unlike the student revolutionaries in China in the ’60s, “they aren’t actually whipping and beheading the intellectuals.” About politics, I’ve never been full of passionate intensity. But on the other hand, I don’t lack all conviction. I just don’t have much appetite for political arguments, especially about words and symbols. I’ve spent hours a day for half my life thinking about language, fiddling and trying to cobble something good with it. I never liked the unending politicized shouting matches about the meaning of this or that word or phrase among people who otherwise don’t give a fuck about language. My father-in-law used to refer to Ash’s and my “civil-war shtick,” the two of us arguing about whether one was coming. The first time I stayed overnight at Ash’s apartment in Providence, in early 2022, I saw a government pamphlet called U.S. Violent Extremist Mobilization Indicators. The following year, when we were having dinner with the about-to-be-famous war correspondent Nicole Stokes, Ash’s ex-girlfriend, he was upset about one of those first-wave right-wing carnival-freak Washington rabble-rousers saying America needed to get a “national divorce,” red states separating from blue. I said to Ash what I always said: “Sweetie? Resist the bait.” And his heroine Nicole agreed with me! She told us the intelligence community’s secret probability score for a U.S. civil war was at the lowest of the five levels between Impossible and Certainty: Almost Certainly Not, somewhere between a 2 and a 12 percent chance. I said the fear and rage among the people I grew up with in the South about library books and drag performers and trans swimmers and the latest elite mots justes back then (cis, Latinx, they instead of he or she) “were just not hills people by the thousands are going to literally die on.” I repeated versions of that for years. During 2036, of course, I stopped being so la-di-da and dismissive. Asher and I could stop arguing about whether it would ever happen, and he could have the greatest told-you-so moment ever. I realize now I had indeed been in denial since I was a teenager wishing and sometimes praying it away. Because I so badly wanted the crazy experiment of the United States of America to continue defying the odds, as always. Now I’m just trying to train myself to experience other people and the world more as I did when I was young. I don’t mean in a childlike way, recovering my innocence or rediscovering my inner girl or any of that. What I mean is how Americans thought of one another until the last 30 years. I’ve always resisted living life electronically, getting mesmerized by screens and software voices. And I really no longer want to be forced to think of everything as a political thing and everyone as a political actor. Obsessive political thinking is like a tic that went horribly viral in the 2010s, a kind of sociopolitical Tourette syndrome. Right before the deadliest viral pandemic in a century, which made the problems of 24/7 digital everything even worse. But instead of lasting a couple of years, this politics illness became a chronic condition. Maybe that’s too metaphorical. But it is a terrible habit, and breaking any bad habit is hard. Such as overeating, which was the bad habit Americans adopted right after weaning themselves off cigarettes and before taking up all the unhealthy bad habits enabled by social media and AI. Next time I talk with Ash, I should ask if he’s proud of me for having a thought that could pass for leftish. But you forgot to include Big Pharma and the gun industry, he’ll say, and probably mention the guns I own, and we’d be at it again. You see? It’s a tough habit to break. Asher says he agrees and that he’s working on it too. He calls it an addiction, a rare figurative use of the word I don’t mind. Although “co-enabling and co-dependent rage addicts,” as he once said of us, was a bit much. But also dark and glamorous, like we’re Kurt Cobain and Courtney Love. Part of my rehab practice is reminding myself of arguments we had over the years that had no political content, the ones that became Things We Can All Laugh About Now. The respite from political talk is no doubt a reason I’ve stayed here in Tennessee instead of moving to San Francisco with Ash. If politicized thinking soured too much of our life, if it became an addiction, then I’m out here for the same reason recovering addicts remove themselves from easy access to drugs and booze and their intimates who use. I hate to admit it, but I’ve found that when I talk with a moving picture of my husband on a screen, I’m much better able to control my impulses to carp and roll my eyes and snort and make political jokes at his expense and get into dumb, unnecessary fights. Even though escaping technomania is a reason I’m here too. My biggest regret has been missing out on all these months of our daughter, Logan, who decided to live with her father. Of course, I’ve told myself, my day-to-day helicoptering utility to a 17-year-old is delimited and diminishing. Which is a lame rationalization. I know one of my main emotions from now on will be missing my children; it occurred to me that returning here may be a self-punishing head start on that with Logan, some sick parental version of breaking up with people before they dump you. Anyhow, what I said earlier about the half-life of the war’s traumas is true. But what to me feels even better is the fading of what preceded it—the compulsive politicization of everything, the fear and hatred of the Other Side as an animating, irresistible everyday emotion. The active contempt has become less salient, diminished, including in rural Middle Tennessee. “I don’t disagree about the vibe shift, honey,” says my local best friend, Victoria, who grew up around here and lives 15 minutes away. “But isn’t it also true for you right now because you don’t have Asher around to bicker with so much?” At first I found the term Disunion too clinical and wonky. But it is more correctly mutual than secession. And has some history, dignity, a touch of grandeur. I’m a fan of all of those. But I call it the breakup. It’s a little glib, but simple, factual, neutral, idiomatic, and broad enough to cover the whole era, beginning to end. It’s also a universally familiar concept, unlike Disunion. It’s relatable. Americans have been strange bedfellows in a mixed marriage for a long time. We grew apart. We stayed together, but finally came to find each other impossible. We fought. We had irreconcilable differences. We split. In a world where half the marriages unravel or rot or die of thirst or blow up. Or do whatever mine is doing. A clichéd question about America since I was a kid concerned our national political “fever,” if and when it would ever break. The fever broke. Along with our beautiful, uncomfortable, brittle, breakable antique national governing arrangements, now being rebuilt and refurbished. This story is adapted from Kurt Andersen’s The Breakup: A Novel. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.
