The Man Who Refused to Sit on the Sidelines
2026-08-11

The Man Who Refused to Sit on the Sidelines

The Atlantic | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
To a firefighter who is inside one, a fire creates as much darkness as heat. Flames start bright but throw off smoke that can swathe everything in pitch black. Lieutenant Kevin Dowdell did not fight fires so much as he groped through and problem-solved them, relying on half-blind judgment and expertise—and his fellow firefighters to keep flaming debris off his back. At the cindering end of a successful 24-hour shift with the New York City Fire Department, Kevin would hang up his helmet, so ash-rubbed that it looked salty, and drive home to his wife and two sons in Rockaway Beach, Queens. “Oh, we had a good job last night,” he’d say, elated as well as exhausted. By “good,” his family knew, he meant the fire had been big. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. In his innermost firefighter’s heart, Kevin liked it when the alarms went off. A good job broke the tedium of drills and doughnut-dunking in the firehouse kitchen. It called on Kevin’s whole mind and body; he had to calculate burn rates and airflow while wielding 40 pounds of gear. Fire needs four elements: oxygen, heat, fuel, and a chemical reaction. Deprive a fire of even one of those, and it goes out. But in New York—a steel-stacked, deep-tunneled metropolis with architecture ranging from tenements to glass towers—the interaction of the elements is complex. One of the questions on the test to become a lieutenant: What is the expansion factor of a 100-foot steel beam as it reaches the inherent heat level of 1,200 degrees Fahrenheit? A firefighter had to be part chemist, part engineer, and part daredevil. But the mission itself was simple. “You show up on someone’s worst day and try to make it better,” Kevin told his sons, Patrick and James. The FDNY had a saying: There were people who just worked for the department for a paycheck, and then there were firefighters. Kevin was a firefighter. “They love to go to a fire,” his widow, RoseEllen, told me, sitting at Patrick’s kitchen table in Rockaway Beach. “They’re waiting for a good fire.” But on September 11, 2001, Kevin met a fire that looked like hell’s own flames. It burned a quarter of a mile up in the sky, unreachable by any ladder, almost at the top of the World Trade Center’s Twin Towers. Kevin’s unit, Rescue 4 in Queens, arrived about 20 minutes after the second hijacked plane had hit. He would have read the color of the smoke and picked apart the elements of the fire: aviation fuel, combustible office materials, and an oxygen-rich chimney effect. They did not make for a good fire. They made for an uncontainable one. Nevertheless, Kevin led his men into the South Tower. Twenty-five years after the attacks, “Never Forget” is a T-shirt slogan. A third of the American public is too young to recall that day. Anniversaries have become rote rituals in which politicians describe scenes they did not witness and national unity that no longer exists. To get beyond the benumbed abstraction, it’s necessary to remember with specificity. September 11 was a lived event. One way to recall it meaningfully, then, is through a life. Kevin Dowdell was handsome in a regular way, with dark-brown hair in an old-fashioned side-comb and a lean triangle of a face that tapered to a good chin. But he had some gravel in him, and his forearms were as thick as the fat end of a baseball bat. He was the grandson of an Irish longshoreman who’d left a dying fishing port in Fingal and arrived at Ellis Island in 1904 with $3 in his pocket. Kevin’s father, a butcher, raised seven children on leftover baloney ends in an 1,100-square-foot rowhouse in Brooklyn. At the age of 18, Kevin went to work as a “sandhog,” the nickname for miners who dug the city’s maze of tunnels that carry cars, steam, sewers, water. He spent his early manhood descending hundreds of feet into compression chambers, which sometimes caused the “staggers” or “chokes,” as sandhogs called the bends. He dynamited through rock and dug out muck up to his knees—slow, grimy work so hazardous that it was said to kill a man for every mile. But a four-hour shift paid as if you’d worked eight. When he wasn’t sandhogging he was at Rockaway Beach, where he surfed and hung out in Jacob Riis Park with a crowd from his old Brooklyn neighborhood. Among them was RoseEllen Fedele, a tall, slim 18-year-old college student. At first he was too shy to make eye contact, but he eventually got her number. They dated for five years, which was how long it took him to get out of sandhogging—and she never doubted that he would. Between shifts he studied for and took every civil-service exam in the city. In 1980, Kevin qualified to be a police officer, and he married RoseEllen, who had become a schoolteacher. He spent just a year wearing a badge before he transferred to the fire department, intrigued by a brother-in-law’s stories about how satisfying the job was. RoseEllen had Patrick on Easter Sunday 1983. When they brought the baby home and relatives crowded to hold him, Kevin refused to let go. “Nobody’s touching him,” he joked. The Dowdells moved to the Rockaways, known as the “Irish Riviera” for its enclaves of firefighters and cops, where the neighbors might swap shifts so you could spend time with your kids. Kevin found a small bungalow that wasn’t winterized and renovated it with his own two hands. They were so poor that they couldn’t afford a stove at first; instead, they cooked on a hot plate. When James came 17 months after Patrick, they put a second crib in the tiny nursery. “You had to, like, slide between them” turned sideways, RoseEllen said. Kevin often worked seven days a week. When he was off duty, he laid hardwood floors and tile to make extra money. If he got home from work and found the boys in bed, sometimes he’d wake them up just to spend a little time with them. “Come on. Let’s take a walk.” They’d stroll down Rockaway Beach to look for old glass bottles, then get an ice cream. He taught them to surf and how to handle the large waves. “Dive to the bottom and grab a fistful of sand. By that time, the wave will have passed over. Trust me.” They did. On Sunday mornings, RoseEllen would simmer red sauce on the stove while she took the boys to Mass, then jar it for the rest of the week’s dinners. One of their few indulgences was Friday dinner in Greenwich Village, usually at a favorite Italian spot at the corner of Carmine and Bleecker Streets called Trattoria Spaghetto. “Put on something nice,” Kevin would tell the boys. “Dress like your father has a job.” He would order the veal parmigiana and a glass of red wine, look around the table at his family, and say, “This is what I work all week for.” The boys spent so much time at the firehouse that they learned the meaning of the crackling radio talk. A “10‑75” was a structure fire in progress, and “K” meant the end of a transmission. The boys would yell, “Rescue 2 to Queens 10‑75 K!” They did drills with Kevin and his team. The big men would strap 10-year-old James into a harness and hoist him over the second-floor firehouse roof. When a call came in, the boys would ride on the front bench seat of the truck, between the men in their bulky turnout coats. They’d get to press the foot pedal that sounded the horn. Kevin showed them how to release it in short blasts. “You have to tap it.” He’d pat James on the knee—the signal for another horn blare. At the site of the fire, the boys would watch through the windshield as the men dismounted in their cumbrous gear and walked through smoking doorways. “The bigger the fire,” James told me, “the bigger the smiles when they came out.” The city had five rescue units, one per borough, and their members were drawn from among the most capable firefighters in the FDNY. Kevin trained for the worst emergencies: tunnel fires, structural collapses, aviation crashes, train derailments. He carried specialized tools and was a skilled black-water diver, for when cars went into rivers. As Rescue 4’s lieutenant, he organized logistics and made swift, expert judgments about risk factors. A manhole cover weighed 300 pounds but could blow six stories into the air. Hidden fuel could be anywhere. An apartment lobby might have an adjacent dry cleaner; grocery stores had stock closets with flammable ingredients. Smoke and heat moved one way in hallways and another in vertical shafts. In a single day, Kevin and his men might pluck a stranded window washer off a building, deal with a transformer fire down a manhole, and fish passengers from the Hudson after their helicopter went down. In 2000, the unit responded to 2,524 calls. But primarily, Rescue 4 went to fires. Sometimes there were half a dozen in a shift. The bigger the fire, the calmer Kevin acted. “He could pull up to the building and tell you right away where the people are gonna be trapped and which way the fire’s gonna spread,” Liam Flaherty, a colleague in Rescue 4, told me. “I think his heart rate actually went down the worse the fire got.” Often Kevin’s men could feel the fire but not see it. “When you’re in this dark environment that’s hot, it’s like being locked in a room with a tiger,” Billy Murphy, another colleague, told me. “You’re like, ‘Where is it?’ ” Flaherty would peer through his breathing mask at an acrid black veil, trying to find his way around a burning apartment. Then he would hear Kevin’s cool voice. “Hey, Li, how we making out over there?” Over the years, Kevin worked so many “good” fires that he became something of a legend in the FDNY, according to Flaherty. In 1991, Kevin helped keep flames at bay on the 12th floor of a Seventh Avenue building to aid a stunning rope rescue of two men from window ledges. In 1993, he was among the first responders to the terrorist bombing of the World Trade Center, which left a crater several stories deep and required him to comb through hazardous rubble, still glowing orange, while gas tanks exploded in the underground parking garages. In 1994, a gas leak blew up a diner in Long Island City, dropping the ground floor into the basement and pinning a waitress under slabs of concrete. Kevin and his partners crawled into the wreckage and worked for three hours to free her as piles of debris quaked over them. He took turns holding her hand and shielding her body with his own, in case the slabs caved. They got her out alive, and won medals for that job. In 1995, he was part of an elite FEMA squad deployed in the aftermath of the Oklahoma City bombing to search for survivors and recover bodies. With his record, he could have risen higher in the department. But a captaincy would have required leaving Rescue 4 for another unit in the city, and he wanted to stay. “Better to be a happy lieutenant,” he told RoseEllen. The unit had a strong sense of brotherhood; many of its members played together in the Emerald Society Pipes and Drums, an Irish group within the department that marched in civic events. Flaherty, a tousle-haired raconteur, was their drum major. Murphy, a tall Coast Guard veteran, played the bagpipes. Kevin loved the Pipes and Drums so much that he coaxed his sons to take Irish-music lessons—Patrick, the pipes; James, the snare drum—so they could march in the St. Patrick’s Day parade, alongside Kevin himself on the bass drum. “I work with the best guys in the world,” he would tell his sons. As with every FDNY unit, the heart of Rescue 4 was the firehouse kitchen, where the men ate together, and drank coffee, and debriefed after every incident. It was also the scene of geyser-like laughter, juvenile and often at someone’s expense. If Kevin corrected the pronunciation of his name (it was Dowdell ), they’d call him “Diddle.” A shift was up to 24 hours, and between alarms they had time for pranks. Once, an officer made the mistake of complaining that he didn’t like hot dogs. For the next month, all the unit served for dinner was hot dogs. Hot dogs in barbecue sauce. Hot dogs in pasta. Hot dogs, grilled and boiled. The officer grimly waited it out. At the end of the month, a large meat loaf landed in the center of the table. Then they cut him a slice. The meat loaf was stuffed with hot dogs. Rescue 4 gave Kevin a sense of home, and a feeling that he was personally protecting his beloved Rockaways. He didn’t just respond to trouble in the area—he sought it out. After a boy in their neighborhood fell into a septic tank, Kevin installed a scanner on his dashboard; he believed he could have helped if he’d heard the call. He’d listen to emergency dispatches while driving home from Little League and say, “We’re close by. Let’s just go check it out.” Kevin would pull over if he saw a car accident and extricate someone from the wreck before EMS arrived. He’d tell his sons not to be bystanders. “Don’t sit on the sidelines. Make things happen. Do things.” Once, when Kevin saw smoke coming from a building in their neighborhood, he told the boys to call the fire department and then ran off, grabbed a garden hose, and climbed through a back window to start putting the blaze out himself. He was strict and exacting with his sons. For every minute they were late to something, Kevin docked them half an hour of free time. When they were old enough, he enlisted them in construction work, and didn’t pay them until they could lift heavy loads. “You work like a man, you get paid like a man.” He once called James home from a friend’s house because he hadn’t cleaned his room. He marched James into the doorway and said, “Look at that.” There was a pair of socks on the floor. Patrick was growing into a burly achiever who aspired to West Point. James was slighter, milder, but with a distance runner’s stamina. During long car rides in New York gridlock, Kevin would ask the boys what they intended to do with their lives. Their grandfather was a butcher. Their father was a fireman. “I want you to be the next step up from that,” Kevin told them. “I want you to do something better than me.” By late summer 2001, Kevin had earned 20 citations for outstanding performance. He was beginning to think about retirement, and he dreamed of traveling—to Iceland in particular, with its volcanic landscape forged in fire. And the boys were nearly men. Patrick was 18 and had just started at Iona University, in New Rochelle, having been wait-listed at West Point. James was turning 17 and starting his senior year at Xavier High School in Manhattan. He wanted to be a firefighter. “You’re going to college,” Kevin insisted, but he took James on a scuba-training run under the Throgs Neck Bridge. James donned an FDNY dry suit and plunged into the dark, roiling East River while his father supervised from the boat. After the dive, someone took a picture of the two: Kevin’s hand is clapped on his son’s shoulder, and James, shrugging off the suit, is grinning widely. On Sunday, September 9, the family celebrated James’s birthday. Kevin had the day off and Patrick came down from Iona, and they barbecued and ate cake. Kevin and Patrick cloistered themselves in the den to work on his reapplication to West Point. Then Kevin drove Patrick back to school and kissed him on the cheek. “Love you. See you soon. I’ll talk to you next week.” Kevin reported to Rescue 4 at 6 p.m. on the evening of September 10 for a 24-hour shift. Liam Flaherty went off duty and headed to band practice at the Elks Lodge. Billy Murphy was on vacation. The kitchen was quiet. At 8:46 the next morning, American Airlines Flight 11 made a terrifying yeering sound as it accelerated into the North Tower of the World Trade Center. Rescue 4’s summons came at 8:55. Seven men hopped on the rig with Kevin: Terrence Farrell, William Mahoney, Peter Nelson, Durrell Pearsall, Peter Brennan, Allan Tarasiewicz, and a potential recruit named Michael Cawley. The ride probably took about 20 minutes. As they barreled toward Lower Manhattan, an unnerving alert came over the dispatch radio: “Be advised we have jumpers.” From a distance, Rescue 4 would have seen the smoke surging from floors 93 to 99 of the North Tower, the tallest building in New York. About seven minutes into their drive, they might have seen the blooming explosion of the second jet, United Airlines Flight 175, as it tilted into the South Tower between floors 77 and 85. Rescue 4 arrived at a scene that was too much for the senses. A torrent of debris fell from the buildings—shredded metal and insulation, chunks of concrete, shivering glass shards—amid an earsplitting clamor of sirens and screaming. Torsos, luggage, and aluminum scraps of 767s were strewn on the sidewalks. What looked like splashes of red paint on the plazas were the remains of jumpers who had pinwheeled from the upper floors of the North Tower. Kevin ran into a stunned fellow lieutenant named Robert LaRocco, from Ladder 9, who had seen a severed forearm with a perfectly manicured hand. LaRocco managed to get a call through to his wife. He told her, “It’s raining people.” Kevin reported to a command post across the street from the North Tower, where the FDNY’s chief of special operations command, Ray Downey, was doling out orders. He assigned Rescue 4 to help with evacuations in the South Tower and, if possible, to get upstairs and beat back the fire. Kevin was seen lining up his men and talking to them intently. He then led them single file toward the South Tower, hugging the sides of buildings. A rescue squad would have had its hands full in the lobby alone. Elevators were broken, stairwells smoked, and radios were unreliable. People were streaming down, badly burned or bloody, and some had to be carried. One elevator had stopped just short of the ground floor with people trapped inside. Firefighters pried open the outer doors and could see their legs, but couldn’t find a way to lower the car enough to free them. Flaming jet fuel cascaded down the shaft as the crews worked. At 9:52 a.m., about half an hour after Kevin had arrived, a battalion chief named Orio Palmer reached the South Tower’s 78th floor, where the jet’s wing had inflicted mass casualties. “We’re going to need two engines up here,” Palmer radioed down. Wherever Kevin was—trying to force open elevators, or starting the slow ascent to 78—the elements of the fire were taking over. The second plane had struck the South Tower at more than 500 miles an hour, stripping the fireproofing from steel trusses and columns. The inferno fed on carpets, curtains, chairs, desks, computers. Plumes of soot trapped the heat. The temperature hit 1,800 degrees Fahrenheit, softening the bones of the building. At 9:59 a.m., the top of the South Tower sagged. Then it began to collapse. Beams and struts slammed into one another. As each floor failed, the falling mass increased, picking up velocity. About 500,000 tons of concrete and steel sped to the earth, reaching an estimated 124 miles an hour. Lieutenant LaRocco of Ladder 9 had just put his foot on a stalled escalator on the north end of the South Tower’s mezzanine when he felt something like a wave in the floor. He heard a mounting roar, like a space shuttle taking off. The light in the windows vanished, as if someone had yanked a giant shade down. Kevin would have had little time to think about the sound above him. It took just 10 seconds for the tower to fall. As the mass neared the ground, it generated a huge blast of compressed air and seismic waves equal to those produced by a 2.1-magnitude earthquake. Paperwork from the towers drifted miles away, littering the beaches of the Rockaways. For most of that day, RoseEllen had been at P.S. 312, preoccupied with getting her third graders back to their parents. Now, at home, she waited for Kevin to call. He always did, leaving a message when she didn’t answer. “Hey, it’s me. If you see anything on the news, don’t worry about it. I’m okay.” But 6 p.m. came and went without any word. At 12:30 a.m., Special Operations Command called and told RoseEllen that Kevin was listed among the missing; all of the guys on Rescue 4’s rig were unaccounted for, as was their captain, Brian Hickey, who had been working with Rescue 3 that day. RoseEllen stayed up all night, her eyes darting between the television news and the front door. Around sunrise, RoseEllen went onto the porch and stared down the street. “At that point, I knew,” she would say, years later. “In my head, I knew.” After a week, one of the crews digging through the pulverized wreckage at Ground Zero came across a Halligan bar, which might have been used to claw at an elevator door. It was marked with KD and R4. Kevin Dowdell, Rescue 4. The tool had been blown out of the lobby. But there was no other sign of Kevin. The remaining men of Rescue 4 set up a war room in the firehouse, manned round-the-clock shifts to dig through the wreckage, and assigned a firefighter to liaise with each family who was missing someone. “Did we find him today?” RoseEllen would ask. No, Billy Murphy would say, day after day. Whenever the door to the Dowdell house opened, three faces would swivel toward it with hope. “We were like, ‘If anyone got out of there or could get out of there, it’s him,’ ” Patrick recalled, but it was always a relative or neighbor. Two weeks after the attacks, RoseEllen decided that sitting around the house in their grief was not doing them any good. “Listen,” she said to the boys. “You’re going back to school, and I’m going back to work.” On the drive to Iona, she persuaded Patrick to finish his reapplication to West Point. “You will not—we will not—use anything as an excuse in your life,” she said. Xavier High School was steeped in mourning; 60 alumni and close relatives of students and staff were lost. Counselors and priests watched James for signs of depression, but when RoseEllen pushed him to see a therapist, he protested. “All I do is talk to people,” he told her. He would channel his emotions into running, sometimes 12 miles at a time. Back at Iona, Patrick couldn’t concentrate. He called James and suggested that they go to Ground Zero to search for their father’s remains. “If we find him, I want to be there,” Patrick told James. “I want to see him. I want to help carry him out.” James heard his father’s voice in his head. Don’t sit on the sidelines. Make things happen. Do things. “I’ll meet you there,” James told his brother. Rescue 4 held a meeting about whether to allow them to join the search. They took a vote, and the boys won. Murphy and Flaherty geared them up with fire boots, gloves, Carhartt suits, helmets, and tools. The guys worried about giving the boys nightmares. Murphy warned them: “If we come across somebody, I’ll ask you to step away until we find out who it is.” The pile, as it was called, was eight stories of contorted metal, sticking up like giant fork tines, spread over 16 smoldering acres. The burning electrical smell was sickening. As Patrick and James clambered through it, they could feel heat in their boot soles. Grapplers lifted steel from the smoking heaps, and the diggers showed the boys what to look for underneath: a boot, a piece of helmet, the reflective tape from a turnout coat. But the main sign of human remains was the odor of decay. As soon as the diggers smelled it, they would wave at the machines to stop. The first time the boys were present for a recovery, the crew cast sidelong looks at James and Patrick. The men and boys removed their helmets. A chaplain was called. They stood at attention as the remains were carried away on a flag-draped stretcher. For seven months, James and Patrick worked at Ground Zero as much as they could. “Being away from it became harder than being there,” Patrick recalled. When his classes at Iona were done for the day, he’d find a ride to the city and dig half the night, then return to campus at 3 or 4 a.m. slit-eyed from ash and lack of sleep, stowing his dusty gear in a garbage bag in his dorm. James kept his Carhartts and tools in his Xavier locker. He would shed his school uniform—blue oxford shirt, necktie, insignia-patched sweater—and tromp out the schoolhouse door, transformed, in coveralls and boots. The crew found parts of helmets, and parts of people. They found impaled bodies that had to be cut away from the steel with torches. They found intact bodies in strange voids; how long had some people survived under the wreckage? Sometimes the boys just put bone fragments in buckets. At the firehouse, Patrick and James sat around the kitchen table debriefing like members of the company. They listened to the men of Rescue 4 wrestle with survivor’s guilt. If they had been on duty, they’d have been in the South Tower at 9:59 a.m. Survival was a matter of lefts and rights, as Flaherty said. They posed terrible “What if?” questions. Should the fire commanders have foreseen the towers’ collapse? If so, should they have kept men from going up? “Well, what was the alternative?” Flaherty argued at the table. “There’s people trapped upstairs. We have to go get them.” Could you imagine, he said, if the FDNY had just given up on the towers? “That would have been the worst day in the history of the fire department the other way.” The kitchen conversations were a kind of counseling for the boys. “We had our people, and we just talked amongst ourselves, and that was enough,” Patrick remembered. So were the funerals for firefighters they played at with the Emerald Society Pipes and Drums. They stood by graves in kilts and red tunics that Flaherty had given them, and performed “Amazing Grace” and “Going Home.” Many children of the dead were preschoolers or grade-schoolers. RoseEllen saw that the firefighters were helping to steady her sons. “Is school good?” Flaherty and others would ask the boys while they dug through the pile. “If your grades aren’t good, you can’t be here.” As a picture of Kevin’s movements with Rescue 4 emerged from survivor accounts, the recovery concentrated on a spot near the South Tower elevator shafts. In late October, on a day the boys were not working, a crew found the remains of four men from Rescue 4. But not Kevin’s. A few feet away was a large, burned-out space where fires had raged below the tonnage for weeks. “You recover two guys that were kind of close by to each other in the rubble, and then you don’t get anybody else,” Patrick said. “It’s really hard. You don’t know how that happens, but that’s how it happens.” On the morning of November 12, 2001, when an American Airlines plane accidentally crashed into the Rockaways shortly after takeoff, RoseEllen could see the smoke from the porch. “You are not going over there,” she told James. When Rescue 4 pulled up to the crash site, James was already there in his gear, hosing down a burning house. “You’re one of us now,” a firefighter told him. James, though just 17, took the FDNY entrance exam and passed. By spring, the pile had become a pit, with almost 1.8 million tons of debris removed. The site was practically broom-clean. The recovery process was down to DNA testing of the smallest fragments. Even then, no Kevin. “There’s nothing there,” Murphy told RoseEllen. “It’s over.” They held Kevin’s memorial service on April 20, 2002. His casket was filled with dust from the spot where they believed he had died, and mementos of him: his extra turnout gear, a pair of fire boots, a copy of the Irish Voice newspaper, travel brochures for Iceland. Letters from his sons and siblings. Hundreds of people attended, including the mayor. Patrick played a bagpipe solo of “Amazing Grace” as the casket was carried into the church. “He was a teacher, mentor, tradesman, listener, and my best friend,” Patrick said during his eulogy, concluding with an Irish blessing that Kevin had recited to him: “May the best days of your past be the worst of your future.” The memorial service marked an abrupt end to months of mission. “It was so consuming from September to April that after it was all said and done, there was a sense of ‘Now what do we do?’ ” James recalled. Kevin was irrecoverable. But they had his voice. Don’t sit on the sidelines. Patrick had just been accepted to West Point, and in June he entered “Beast Barracks,” the academy’s rigorous six-week cadet-induction program. It made the news: 9/11 firefighter’s kid joins the War on Terror. Overnight, he went from friends consoling him to officers barking orders. He marched to breakfast, marched to lunch, and spent nights shining his shoes. What if he couldn’t cut it? He feared letting his father down. He refused to buy his mother a West Point sweatshirt. “I didn’t want it to be a constant reminder of my failure if I didn’t make it through,” Patrick said. The combination of mourning, loneliness, and self-doubt made him mutinous. He racked up punishment tours for lateness and absences, and was made to march in circles carrying a rifle for five hours at a time. He would sneak out to bars to meet old friends from the Rockaways. He felt like he was trying to break free from prison. He considered quitting, to escape the pressure. RoseEllen felt powerless and wished for her husband. Patrick had relied on Kevin. “I didn’t know anything about the Army,” she said. “I couldn’t help him.” Flaherty stepped in. “Bro, failure is not an option,” he told Patrick. “You cannot—you cannot—drop out.” He warned him that quitting would alter the trajectory of his life. Patrick rallied, though not before he missed a 9/11 anniversary ceremony because he was walking another punishment tour. This time, it was about a girl: He’d met Katie Neudeck at a party in Rockaway Beach at the end of summer break. He was supposed to report back to West Point that day, and his lateness cost him 20 hours of walking. She was motivation to graduate; so were the pictures of his father taped to his dorm-room wall, and a steel bracelet on his wrist etched with LT. KEVIN DOWDELL 9-11-01. From the September 2021 issue: Jennifer Senior on what Bobby McIlvaine left behind James couldn’t formally enter the FDNY until he was 21, and at one point talked about joining the Marines. “No, you will not,” his mother said. Instead he enrolled at Iona. RoseEllen was alone in the house, but had two sons she still needed to get to the finish line of manhood. Patrick and James were strapping—bigger than their father, all bone and muscle and hard-charging. They had chosen dangerous futures, and there was little she could do about it. “That’s what they knew,” she said. In 2006, when James turned 21, he dropped out of his final semester at Iona to enter the FDNY academy; he then joined Ladder 174, in Flatbush. (He finished college the following year.) That same spring, Patrick graduated from West Point and prepared for deployment to Iraq. “I want to kill terrorists,” he declared to the Associated Press. By 2008, he was a platoon leader on combat patrols in Baghdad. With Patrick skirting roadside bombs and James running into fires, RoseEllen could only pray. Her faith told her that Kevin was still on watch. “I just said, in my head, He’s protecting them,” she recalled. This is what Kevin Dowdell worked all week for: Patrick would make captain in the Army, and James would take his old man’s badge number and carry his Halligan tool. And RoseEllen— who never allowed pain, hers or theirs, to be an excuse—would see them through. While Patrick was serving abroad, James was earning a medal. At 4:30 one morning in March 2010, Ladder 174 responded to a heavy fire in a two-story building. James and another firefighter battled their way to the second floor with a fire extinguisher and, working in high heat and zero visibility, forced open the door of a rear apartment. In the smoke, James found an unconscious nine-month-old on a bed. Their team got four people out of the building alive, including the infant. James joined Rescue 2. He would be working with some of Kevin’s former colleagues, and would be expected not to drag the anchor chain, as they said in the department about slackers. In 2017, while working under Flaherty, James set the unofficial FDNY record for a rope rescue when he descended 200 feet down an unlit Con Edison shaft to help workers trapped in an underground elevator. “I was just the dope on the rope,” he said, crediting his partners who’d made the rig. Early last year, he was promoted to lieutenant—his father’s rank. He’s now studying for the captain’s exam. After Patrick left the Army as an assistant operations officer for an artillery battalion, he thought about joining the FDNY. He scored first on the entrance test, out of about 45,000 applicants, but again heard his father’s voice: I want you to do something better than me. Patrick didn’t think there was any vocation greater than firefighter, but he opted for a career in business. He works in cybersecurity in Manhattan, and his home is on the bayside of Rockaway Beach, where he can look directly at One World Trade Center and the twin light towers that shine every September 11. Each year on the anniversary, RoseEllen and her sons return to Rescue 4’s firehouse for a memorial ceremony. Firefighters gather in their dress uniforms. They bow their heads for a moment of silence in front of a plaque bearing the names of the dead. Invariably a pedestrian will pause in the firehouse driveway and say, casually, “What’s going on? Is there a parade or something?” James or Patrick will raise his head and say, “It’s September 11.” After the memorials, the Dowdells return to the Rockaways for a family dinner. They leave the seat at the head of the table open, with a glass of red wine in front of it. At the end of their meal, they pass around the glass, and each person tells a story about Kevin. Among those seated at the family table is a retired firefighter named Tom O’Day, whom RoseEllen began seeing in 2010 (“He understood everything,” she said). When Hurricane Sandy hit the Rockaways, RoseEllen evacuated but Tom stayed to save her possessions; he carried every memento of Kevin he could find above the flood line. In 2019, Tom and RoseEllen went to Iceland, where she buried a Mass card from Kevin’s memorial service on a remote beach, placed a rock over it, and noted the coordinates. Five grandchildren, ages 5 through 13, are also at the family table, as are Patrick’s and James’s wives, Katie and Chrissy. They all live within walking distance of RoseEllen and the old home, where an oil portrait of Kevin hangs in the front hall. The grandchildren have been going to firehouses since they were born. As they run around, climbing and sliding down the fire pole, they talk about Grandpa Kevin as if they knew him. Recently the youngest grandchild, Carly, sat with RoseEllen, looking across Jamaica Bay. “I see Grandpa Kevin’s building,” she said. “What happened to him?” RoseEllen explained again about the bad guys who flew planes into the buildings and made them fall. Read: Making 9/11 relevant to young learners That day, reflexive acts of duty turned a terrible event into a survivable one for many people. In the aftermath, firehouses cared for the families of the fallen with something beyond duty. Beyond duty: two grieving boys hunting for their lost father and instead recovering other people’s fathers, and playing hymns for them. A firehouse full of rowdy, imperfect men finishing the job of raising them, and giving them the balm of purpose. The FDNY lost 343 men on September 11; 65 of their sons and daughters now serve in the department. Twenty-five years later, Kevin is still missing. James’s promotion to lieutenant fell on what would have been his 70th birthday. “Never Forget” is no slogan to the Dowdells. “We live it, and we live with it,” Patrick said. Living without Kevin is a form of bravery, of course—a fire all its own. Living his example is how they find their way out of the smoke. This article appears in the September 2026 print edition with the headline “Kevin Dowdell’s Boys.”

Main outcomes measures Among 443 occupations studied, percentage of deaths attributed to Alzheimer’s disease for taxi drivers and ambulance drivers and each of the remaining 441 occupations, adjusting for age at death and other sociodemographic factors.We then used multivariable logistic regression at the individual level to estimate risk adjusted percentages of deaths from Alzheimer’s disease for each occupation, adjusting for age at death (indicator variables for each year of age), sex (male or female), race and ethnic group (black, white, Hispanic, Asian or Pacific Islander, other race), and educational attainment (indicator variables for 8th grade, 9-12th grade, high school, college, master’s or doctorate). Mortality odds ratios from the multivariable model were also ranked from lowest to highest across occupations to facilitate identification of occupations with the lowest risk of death from Alzheimer’s disease; chief executive (occupation code: 0010) was arbitrarily chosen as the reference group in regression models. We performed four sensitivity analyses to assess the robustness of our comparisons... Would you look at that, Slashdot skeptic! News and Politics - Slate Magazine
A Man Burned Alive in My New York Prison Last Month. Why Is Nobody Talking About It?
2026-08-11

A Man Burned Alive in My New York Prison Last Month. Why Is Nobody Talking About It?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story each week about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back. You’ll also receive updates on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. “Help me!” shouted Marcos “Bori” Alcaraz. “I’m on fire!” Thirty-five-year-old Alcaraz, a slim kid from the Bronx serving time for manslaughter, stood pressed against the steel bars inside South Hall—an infamous cellblock at Eastern NY Correctional Facility, a maximum-security prison tucked away in Napanoch, New York. It was around 1:40 a.m. on a summer Saturday last month when Alcaraz’s cell ignited into an inferno. “It looked like the sun was inside his cell,” said Akbar “Block” McClennon, 39, a fellow student in the prison’s college program who was locked up on the same gallery as Bori. Block is a tall, dark-skinned Black man with locs and striking, expressive eyes. Block described staring up at the gallery wall, transfixed by the horror. He saw Bori’s flickering silhouette gripping the bars. “He was shouting for help for, like, 30 minutes. Then there was a flash, and you didn’t see him standing on the gate anymore.” Fire dominates today’s headlines. The world itself feels like it’s burning. This horrifying story of a man burned alive in one of the biggest states in the union has barely made a blip in the news, though. The details of Alcaraz’s death are truly horrifying. Severe burns trigger immediate, catastrophic nervous shock, and because the skin is the body’s largest organ, few can survive a third-degree burn covering half their frame. Burning to death is universally recognized as one of the most excruciating ways to die. But burning to death inside a prison cell—a concrete tomb—is a distinct nightmare. Once you are ablaze, escape is impossible. There is no room to stop, drop, and roll. In a fire, any person expects to hear the piercing blare of a smoke alarm, yet not a single detector sounded that night. As an incarcerated person, you find that your life rests entirely in the hands of prison employees; you are completely dependent on them to hear your pleas and take immediate, lifesaving action. Given these facts and the situation on the ground in New York correctional facilities, it seems Alcaraz never stood a chance. I am currently serving my 17th year for manslaughter, and reporting on systemic issues within the criminal legal system from the inside out. To trace the breadcrumbs of this tragedy, readers need critical context. On Dec. 9, 2024, Robert Brooks was fatally beaten by officers at Marcy Correctional Facility. The release of the body camera footage by Attorney General Letitia James on Dec. 27 led to the firing of 13 guards and the indictment of six by a grand jury. That indictment triggered an illegal strike by corrections officers. In response, Gov. Kathy Hochul deployed the National Guard, who now occupy facilities as a volunteer-only, long-term force. New York State Department of Corrections and Community Supervision Commissioner Daniel F. Martuscello III initiated an aggressive recruitment drive, bringing waves of rookie guards into the system. Yet, despite this surge of security personnel, basic living conditions have only decayed. Over the past year alone, six men have died inside South Hall: overdoses, a homicide, and, now, a horrific death by fire. South Hall is known as one of Eastern’s “dump-off” blocks, where they send rabble-rousers, the outspoken, argumentative prisoners, the ones who have most fallen out of favor with the guards. Most prisons have one; Eastern has two: B-Block and South Hall. Aside from an unlucky draw, there is a short list of reasons a prisoner ends up here. It is also where staff send men they label legitimate troublemakers—those with heavy disciplinary records. Other times, a sergeant holds a personal grudge, or the officers on your cellblock simply don’t like you. They contact Movement and Control, and the next morning, an officer looks down at his clipboard and barks: “Pack your things. You’re moving to South Hall.” When you first step into South Hall, to your immediate right is “the bubble”—a barred enclosure fitted with chairs, a desk, a telephone, and a microwave. Behind it sits an officers’ bathroom, and tucked behind that is another room, humorously referred to by us prisoners as “The Boom-Boom Room,” for its mattress and all that it might imply. If you turn left, you walk onto Nine Company; directly above it sits 10 Company. The cellblock towers four tiers high, each tier stretching across 28 cells. Alcaraz was locked on 14 Company—the side facing the parking lot—inside Cell 20, all the way in the back on the third tier. When the on-duty officers heard the desperate cries for help, why didn’t they jump up and sprint to Alcaraz’s aid? The answer is more sinister and complex than simple laziness or burnout. Over the past few years, staff have become desensitized to prisoners smoking synthetic cannabinoids like K2, often laced with fentanyl, which cause men to have “episodes,” or “eps.” An episode is prison slang for a severe hallucination. (In a previous facility, I watched a man having an ep in which he was convinced he was Spider-Man; when staff arrived to escort him to medical, he was shooting imaginary webs from his wrists.) During these episodes, men collapse or turn violently erratic. When they pass out, they often suffer violent seizures, triggering a code call. A nurse arrives with a gurney, rolls the prisoner to the clinic, and administers Narcan. Once coherent, the man is marched right back to his cellblock. Eps happen almost daily in South Hall, sometimes three times a day. But Alcaraz wasn’t having an ep. He was burning alive. One theory is that officers initially assumed it was just another episode and brushed it off. But a witness told me that a male officer moved past mere apathy, actively shouting at the screaming men to “shut the fuck up” and “stop calling.” During the day shift, corrections officers like to work in South Hall because it’s the cellblock that’s always locked down, which makes it prime real estate for officers who want to spend their 12 hours chilling. At around 8 p.m., night shift workers shut down every gallery in South Hall, locking every prisoner inside his cell, including the porter, who sometimes acts as an additional set of eyes and ears. If the porter is locked in, however, then that shout might not be heard. Once everyone is locked in, COs get to lounge around inside the bubble: eat, drink coffee, flirt with each other, complain, maybe nap until they have to perform the 10:30 p.m. count. Whatever was going on, nobody responded to Alcaraz’s desperate pleas for help and the shouts of the other inmates. In an attempt to shift the blame, prison administrators might be trying to focus on the physical cause of the fire. But whether it was electrical, chemical, or whatever, what killed Alcaraz was the negligence of the guards. When a CO hears someone scream for help, it doesn’t matter if they think someone’s having an episode or playing around; they are supposed to investigate it because, as in Alcaraz’s case, the fire could be real. Witnesses I spoke with were unanimous on one detail: “Only one of the three officers on duty that night tried to do anything.” There are no fire extinguishers stationed on the company itself. Instead, two fire hose cabinets sit in each gallery, one at the front, and one near Cell 24. When officers finally acted, they pulled only half the hose out, leaving the rest coiled inside the cabinet, and stood there baffled as to why the water wouldn’t flow. By the time the nurse finally arrived, Bori was so severely charred that she couldn’t find a spot of unburned skin to insert an IV. I spoke with two South Hall residents, Dario Wynerman and Shevockie Bloodworth, who believed they had suffered smoke inhalation. But when they finally made it down to the clinic, medical staff refused to see them, saying, “We can’t help you.” Standard procedure mandates that anyone suspected of smoke inhalation receives a bronchoscopic examination of their airways. That examination was never performed. If medical personnel were to officially document smoke-inhalation injuries across the block, the Department of Corrections and Community Supervisionwould be exposed to massive liability, both for medical treatment and for the lucrative civil lawsuits mounting on the horizon. It is neglect: pure, willful neglect at every level, from medical to mental health. And Alcaraz was not the only victim. Normally, when a death occurs inside a cellblock, facility executives dispatch clergy, rehabilitation coordinators, and mental health counselors to conduct psychological welfare checks. Men here, some of whom have served decades, are deeply traumatized. Yet no prison staff ever came to speak to a single soul. The week Alcaraz died, I went out to the yard to work out. The heat felt like 100 degrees radiating off the pavement. “I can’t get the image out of my mind … him standing on the gate, screaming, ‘Help me! I’m on fire!’ ” said Richardo “Rick” Daniell, 35, who is serving over a decade for attempted murder. Shirtless, he paced relentless circles on the blacktop. His mind, like his feet, refused to slow down. Alcaraz was one of Daniell’s closest friends. They ate their meals together and walked laps around the yard talking about life. Alcaraz had lived a hard, brutal road, Daniell told me. He had lost his mother during this sentence, but he had finally begun to put his life together before this nightmare struck. Daniell fondly described his friend as “stand-up,” “caring,” and fiercely “loyal.” He pulled out a worn Polaroid of the two of them, side by side, leaning against a concrete wall, Alcaraz facing the camera with the quiet bravado of a man used to taking life’s blows head-on. Ever since the fire, Daniell had carried that photo everywhere in his pocket. “He was a good father too,” Daniell added. When he mentioned that Alcaraz had left behind a 16-year-old daughter, the weight of his death struck me like a blow to the chest. I’m a father to a teenage girl myself, and my heart went out to his family. Missing your daughter’s youth while behind bars is a crushing weight, but knowing she’ll never see her father again is a far darker reality. I couldn’t stop thinking about the devastating grief that young girl is carrying. Daniell stared at me, his eyes burning with indignation. “They told his baby’s mom he felt sick earlier in the day and died in medical.” “That’s fucking crazy,” I said, stunned into silence. The administration’s attempt to stonewall Alcaraz’s family comes as no surprise. His hair was burned off, his clothes fused to his flesh. Alcaraz was unrecognizable. No administrator wants to handle a political crisis this explosive. Two weeks later, I noticed the fire and safety team walking through the prison, holding clipboards, testing alarms, and inspecting extinguishers. Out in the yard, the fire hydrants received a fresh, bright coat of yellow paint. Men in West Wing, the block visible from my window, were even given a fire drill. In my five years across three different prisons, I cannot recall a single authentic fire drill. Usually, staff stage them as a tactical bait and switch: Once the block is cleared under the guise of safety, K9 units and search teams move in to tear through the empty cells. I am thoroughly unimpressed by this sudden, performative show of fire safety. I cannot erase the image of that scorched cell from my mind. All the walls are black. The toilet and sink are black. The bed frame, black. The ceiling, black. Crispy. It doesn’t even look like a cell anymore. It looks like a bomb went off inside. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Right-Wing Reaction to AOC’s Egg Freezing Is Very Revealing
2026-08-11

The Right-Wing Reaction to AOC’s Egg Freezing Is Very Revealing

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Over the weekend, Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez posted a video of herself doing a hormone injection as part of the process of freezing her eggs. “Don’t be weird about this,” she says, “even though I know all of you will be.” And, yes, everyone got super weird about it. AOC is 36, which led much of the right-wing internet (and some of the left-wing internet) to jump on her choice. Why doesn’t she just have a baby with the guy she’s with? Egg freezing at 36 is pointless! Egg freezing doesn’t guarantee you a baby! Having children after 40 is selfish! “She’s glamorizing the lie that women can have it all!” Most of these comments are coming from the same people who disparage childless women, and who disparage single mothers, judge working mothers, and want to pull benefits from mothers who are poor and have children they can’t afford to feed. In the conservative universe, there is only one correct way to be a mother, and that’s to be married at a young age to a breadwinner man upon whom you are wholly dependent, to not work for pay, and to have as many children as possible—but if your husband doesn’t make enough money to support you all, or if he turns out to be a jerk, or if he dies, well, you’re an idiot and a bad mother for being poor/choosing the wrong man/not having a better life plan. The response on the left was largely to leave AOC alone, because her freezing her eggs is none of our business. But she’s a congresswoman who posted about it online—she made it the public’s business. There’s something very powerful there: She’s demystifying a process that can still be shrouded in unnecessary secrecy, and making a choice many women have at least considered. She’s also opening up a broader conversation about the choices and trade-offs adults have to make when it comes to love, life, work, and family. And we can have that conversation without being super weird about it, or about AOC herself. I don’t know why AOC has chosen to freeze her eggs. But I know why many women whom I know have chosen to freeze their eggs. For the most part, they did so because they were not yet in a position where they wanted to have a child but wanted to preserve the possibility in the future. Most of them were not partnered and weren’t sure they wanted to be single mothers. Many weren’t sure they wanted children at all but wanted to leave the possibility open—with the full knowledge that egg freezing is far from any sort of guarantee. AOC has been engaged to her long-term partner since 2022. Perhaps they are still together and one or both are just not ready for children, or unsure if they want them at all. Perhaps they aren’t together, and she is doing the math on additional years of dating plus waning fertility and deciding to hedge her bets on what is currently the most reliable if vastly imperfect way to extend one’s fertility. Maybe she wants to run for president and understandably doesn’t want to be pregnant while doing so. We don’t know. What we do know is that AOC is an intelligent woman who can surely read the research on fertility outcomes for women as we age, as well as the literature on what egg freezing can and cannot promise. We can do what feminists encourage, which is to assume that women are the best judges of their own reproductive lives, and in the best position to make decisions about those lives—even if they make choices we think are unwise; even if they make choices they may later regret. The conservative right seems to operate from the assumption that all women must have children, should have them early and often, and should have them only within the context of marriage to a gainfully employed man—and that there are scores of great men around who are eager for marriage and fatherhood in their 20s. In reality, finding a person to spend the rest of your life with can be hard—and, frankly, it should be hard. And it can and should take a while for many of us to reach a point in our lives when we feel ready for marriage. Women’s and men’s expectations for a life partner have both increased as marriage has become more optional and as women have realized more opportunities to support themselves. Conservatives may not like that, but “Make marriage compulsory, unhappy, and about economic survival instead of love again” is not exactly a popular position. Many couples may wish they had met earlier in life, and some people marry their high school sweethearts, but I don’t know all that many single adults who wish they had married the person they dated at 17. I do, however, know a great many people who spent their 20s building meaningful careers, forging important friendships, dating enough to learn what did and did not work for them romantically, and generally enjoying themselves—and becoming the kinds of people who might feel ready for a lifelong financial and romantic commitment, which is no small thing. A great many women make use of today’s incredible reproductive technologies not because they’re stereotypical Career Women casting men aside in pursuit of the corner office, but because they want pretty simple things that haven’t materialized yet: a loving relationship with someone who would also make a good husband and father; a life that feels stable enough to bring children into it. No woman can snap her fingers and make the right man and a stable life materialize (and many women, at many times in our lives, don’t want the right man to materialize). But if she has the resources, she can freeze her eggs and perhaps buy herself a little time. AOC is one of the most prominent politicians in the United States. She has been in Congress for the past seven years, and has made a name for herself as one of its most diligent, intelligent, and effective members. She has helped draw an entire generation of progressives into politics, and totally changed the game for young people, young women, and democratic socialists. If she wants a baby, I hope she has one. But her life has also been pretty meaningful as is—and she’s been able to accomplish what she has precisely because she’s been able to make her own choices about whether to have children. Her egg-freezing decision is a part of that continuum. The critics are right that egg freezing doesn’t guarantee you a baby. But I suspect that women know as much. It’s also true that the egg-freezing industry has an incentive to overpromise and oversell. But there is also just simply much we don’t know about a technology that can have a 10- or 20-year horizon—that is, if a woman freezes her eggs at 20, researchers need to wait a few decades before they can answer the question of whether she’ll ever thaw them, and what will happen next. The data on egg freezing remains mixed. One meta-analysis found that of more than 13,500 patients who went through egg-freezing cycles, only about 10 percent returned to use those frozen eggs. The rest ostensibly had children some other way, or chose not to have children at all. Of the 10 percent who retrieved their frozen eggs, 34 percent became pregnant, and 29 percent had a live birth. That means that most women who froze their eggs did not wind up with a baby from those eggs (which, to be clear, is not the same as them not winding up with a baby at all). This information is useful for women to have, and the fertility industry should be required to be transparent with patients. But it’s also not the same as saying that egg freezing is pointless or doesn’t work, which seems to be the narrative adopted by many on the political right. The analysis also found that “the most widely reported reason for fertility preservation is single relationship status or the lack of a partner, particularly one who is willing or committed to having children”—which suggests that many women who froze their eggs eventually did find a willing and committed partner and went on to have children naturally; a small minority went on to have children using their thawed eggs either with a partner or on their own; and others likely did not find a willing and committed partner and decided not to have children at all. Any of these outcomes are possible for AOC and any other woman who freezes her eggs. I would imagine she knows that no single outcome is guaranteed and there is no way to live a life totally insulated from regret or misstep. But in being the person in charge of this decision, she is at least making the best decision for her given the information she has and the desires that she feels, even if she can’t guarantee where it will lead. This is, in essence, what feminists have long wanted for women: not perfect or frictionless lives; not the false promises of fairy tales; just the very real adult work of having to consider what you want and make choices given what you have. Brett Kavanaugh Opened the Door for Trump’s Latest Supreme Court Defiance
2026-08-11

Brett Kavanaugh Opened the Door for Trump’s Latest Supreme Court Defiance

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story each week about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back. You’ll also receive updates on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Last week, President Donald Trump continued his defiant stance against the Supreme Court’s birthright citizenship decision from earlier this summer, firing off two new executive orders about which people born in the U.S. should be declared citizens. Despite losing at the Supreme Court on the legal issue in late June, the Trump administration remains committed to twisting a long-settled constitutional question into a tool to keep Black and brown people out of American life. The conservative legal movement, at first skeptical of Trump’s original executive order attacking birthright citizenship, has now fully embraced the cause. And the cause’s survival can all be traced back to our Supreme Court’s unwillingness to give the Trump administration the overwhelming legal defeats it deserves. In Trump v. Barbara, the court struck down Trump’s first executive order on birthright citizenship. Signed on his first day in office, “Protecting the Meaning and Value of American Citizenship” purported to strip the right to be a U.S. citizen from children born to parents who are in the country unlawfully, as well as parents lawfully but only temporarily in the country. This was in spite of the fact that the 14th Amendment grants birthright citizenship to “all persons” who are born in the United States and subject to its laws. All persons. The only exceptions to this rule that have ever been consistently recognized are for people technically born within the country’s borders but under the legal authority of a separate sovereign, such as children of foreign diplomats. It’s this exception that Trump’s first executive order tries to capitalize on. Trump barely took the effort to even change the name of the order the court had struck down, calling the new one “Continuing to Protect the Meaning and Value of American Citizenship.” This new order seeks to expand the number of people who fall under the foreign diplomat exception. People who would no longer get citizenship would include children whose parents have been named as part of a designated terrorist organization, are foreign government officials, work for foreign-based nonprofits, or are involved with certain international business transactions. The order stretches the birthright citizenship exception as it’s been traditionally recognized beyond recognition. As the American Civil Liberties Union explained in its aftermath last week, “Birthright citizenship is guaranteed by the Constitution. No additional executive order can change the meaning of the Constitution.” Trump’s second executive order focuses on a related citizenship issue: “Ending Birth Tourism.” A longtime pet project of the president, birth tourism is the practice in which companies help foreigners enter the United States, usually under visa applications, for the express purpose of having children in the U.S. who would then be citizens. There are no statistics establishing birth tourism as a wide-ranging problem. At oral argument, Solicitor General John Sauer could only provide anecdotes about newspaper clippings from China, but admitted that “no one knows” the true number. Unbothered by his solicitor general’s concession, Trump continues to repeat with no basis that hundreds of thousands of birth tourists have given birth in this country. Never mind that tourists account for less than 2 percent of all United States births—and that 2 percent figure is all tourism, even before you make potentially ill-founded judgments about whether someone committed fraud to visit this country. The birth-tourism executive order, if it holds up in court, seems likely to lead to frequent ill-founded judgments. First of all, committing fraud in visa applications is already illegal. Border agents already have the authority to question a foreigner’s reason for applying for a visa or entering the country. And in case there’s any question about whether this order might be applied evenhandedly across foreign countries, remember that this is the same administration that is currently taking almost exclusively white South Africans as refugees. And Section 5 of the order allows the secretary of state and Homeland Security to grant any exemptions they want to parents who might otherwise fall within the order’s orbit. All in all, then, the order takes a building of preexisting visa laws and simply paints it over with a coat of whiteness. To understand how these new executive orders push the boundaries of both U.S. and international law, it’s worth taking a moment to appreciate just how deeply ingrained birthright citizenship is in our country’s history. Chief Justice John Roberts’ opinion for the court explained how the long-held understanding of birthright citizenship dates back to the English crown. The idea that nearly everyone born on the land became a citizen was so pervasive as to have a name: jus soli, or “the right of the soil.” Legal scholars in the early 1800s treated this right as both intact and broad. In the words of Supreme Court Justice Joseph Story: When it comes to birthright citizenship, “nothing is better settled.” But in the infamous case of Dred Scott v. Sandford, the Supreme Court did an about-face and declared that formerly enslaved Black Americans were not citizens, even though they were born here. That case wouldn’t just go down as one of the most shameful moments in the court’s history; it was also the legal system’s most significant contribution in devolving the country into the Civil War. Dred Scott weighed heavily on the mind of the reunited country that passed both the 14th Amendment and the Civil Rights Act of 1866, both of which recognized citizenship as a birthright for virtually all persons born in this country. And a later case about a child of Chinese parents, United States v. Wong Kim Ark, confirmed that the 14th Amendment’s citizenship clause applied to people of all races. It’s not unusual for President Trump to try to ignore or twist a legal ruling on an issue he lost on. The better question is why he thinks the effort will succeed. After all, there are other areas where Trump has quietly retreated after a court loss or public outcry. The reason the president persists is that despite all this history, text, and precedent, only five of the nine justices read the 14th Amendment as protecting birthright citizenship in Barbara. The narrowness of that margin was and continues to be a scandal. Justice Brett Kavanaugh sided with Justices Neil Gorsuch, Clarence Thomas, and Samuel Alito in saying they would rule that the constitution does not protect birthright citizenship for children of immigrants who don’t have permanent legal status or are temporary visitors. (Kavanaugh, in a separate opinion joined by nobody, thought the executive order only contradicted a federal statute that Congress could change.) Let’s be clear: This court was one vote away from doing Dred Scott for yet a new group of people who lack political power. All in defense of an order that has been rightly condemned as “blatantly unconstitutional.” Barbara, and now these new orders, is also a symptom of a court that operates in a lopsided manner that favors the administration. This is especially true in the immigration context. In the temporary protected status case, for example, the 6–3 court pulled the rug out from under the Immigration Act of 1990 and the constitutional guarantee of equal protection, letting the Trump administration kick out hundreds of thousands of people from Haiti and other countries. When this court upholds a Trump executive order, it is with the brightest green-means-go signal a majority can muster. But when they rule against the administration, like with Barbara, there isn’t equal force on the other side. Roberts’ opinion for the court, despite being well-written and legally correct, resists critiquing the original order with the forcefulness this issue deserves. And the dissents are littered with both reasons why they believed the Trump administration was right in this case and pathways to future actions that would also be valid in their view. Most obviously, there was Kavanaugh’s road map for a congressional statute that he believed could somehow expand the number of constitutional exceptions to birthright citizenship. Collectively, the lesson that the court gives the administration is clear—just try again. This lopsided approach allows the court to reap the benefits of positive headlines when they rule against the administration, all the while paving a stepping stone for further abuses. This pattern could be seen clearly this term when the court’s conservatives overturned the last remaining plank of the Voting Rights Act that two of them had joined the liberals to uphold just three years ago. Even beyond last week’s orders, conservatives are already meeting to develop a plan to overturn the Barbara ruling altogether. By seeking to overturn Barbara, the anti-birthright legal movement hopes to create a different lopsided relationship between Black and brown children and their government. As the court in Barbara explained, birthright citizenship launches a reciprocal relationship between a person and the sovereign. At birth, the person implicitly agrees to be subject to the laws of the government. In exchange, the government owes that person treatment equal to that of others born on its soil. The Trump administration wants to rupture one half of that relationship. They would like children of immigrants to suffer under fear of violence, imprisonment, and deportation. But they want to relieve themselves of their own obligation to recognize the right of babies who have quite literally only known America from the second they were born. As a businessman who’s spent his life making deals that would screw over the other side, perhaps it’s not surprising that Trump would want to renege on America’s side of the bargain with children of immigrants. What is disheartening, though, is to have a high court that seems on the brink of letting him break that bargain. There have been two eras in our nation’s history in which a coalition of Americans tried to deny birthright citizenship to minorities. In the first era, the coalition temporarily succeeded against Black Americans, leading to a Dred Scott decision that everyone now agrees was an immoral mistake. The second era was against Asian Americans, and that effort failed with a Wong Kim Ark decision that went from settled law to contested precedent in the span of a couple of years. We are now in the third era. Let’s hope that the Barbara decision was the final destination, and not a temporary stop on the way to stripping the right that allows all other rights to flourish. Want to Know Who Comes After Donald Trump? Keep an Eye on Today’s GOP Primary.
2026-08-11

Want to Know Who Comes After Donald Trump? Keep an Eye on Today’s GOP Primary.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-11

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Exactly one month after Sen. Lindsey Graham’s death, GOP primary voters will head to the polls on Tuesday to vote for his replacement. It’ll be their second try this summer to pick a nominee—and it’s unlikely to be their last. The party’s voters nominated Graham in June, shortly before his unexpected death in early July. In theory, the race to nominate his successor could end Tuesday, should one candidate win an outright majority. But with 10 contestants running, it’s more likely that the contest will narrow the field to a two-candidate runoff to be held Aug. 25. The race is unlikely to affect the Senate’s balance of power next year: This is South Carolina, and even if the national environment favors Democrats come November, the GOP nominee is a heavy favorite to win Graham’s old seat. (Democrats have nominated Annie Andrews, a pediatrician from Charleston who lost a House race in 2022.) But that doesn’t mean that the primary is without national importance: It represents not just a rare Senate opening in South Carolina (in the past hundred years, only four men have been elected to this seat) but a rare opportunity to discern the shape of the Republican primary electorate in a key 2028 primary state. It’s an opportunity to observe not only the continued power of Trump’s endorsement but also how much a particular flavor of MAGA-inclined Republican is popular among the party faithful. Of the 10 contenders, four are considered to have a real shot at the nomination. Newly minted incumbent Darline Graham, the sister of Lindsey, was appointed to the seat initially as a placeholder. A political novice, Darline is a blank slate and has been notoriously press-shy since taking office. Although she did interviews with primetime Fox News hosts, she steered clear of local media initially. She has been a halting candidate who has wholeheartedly embraced Trump’s endorsement of her and has differentiated herself from her deceased brother only in loud support of getting rid of the Senate filibuster—one of Trump’s many demands of his party’s Senate caucus. Then, there is former Gov. Mark Sanford, who gained fame as a budget hawk and infamy for his extramarital adventures on the “Appalachian Trail.” After leaving the state House, Sanford served several terms in Congress before losing reelection in his primary after Trump endorsed his opponent. Sanford very briefly mounted a 2020 primary challenge to Trump as well. Finally, there are two sitting congressmen, Reps. Russell Fry and Ralph Norman. Fry was first elected in 2022 as the Trump-backed primary challenger to Tom Rice, a longtime back-bench conservative who voted to impeach the president for his efforts to overturn the 2020 presidential election. The 41-year-old Fry has become a loyal Trump ally since and represents the steady merger between MAGA and the traditional Republican establishment. However, as an incumbent congressman running for reelection, he would leave his House seat vacant for months if elected to the Senate. The result has fed concern that if Republicans somehow manage to win a slim majority in the midterms it would immediately be imperiled by a monthslong vacancy. Norman is a more curious character. A Freedom Caucus member who mounted a losing gubernatorial bid in 2026 before switching to the special Senate election, he seems to have all the qualifications to appeal to the MAGA right. However, he is missing one key asset. Norman endorsed Nikki Haley, a longtime friend and ally, in the 2024 Republican presidential primary. It’s not the type of move that endears a politician in Trump’s orbit. The conventional wisdom is that Darline Graham will likely make the runoff and then the second spot will be a competition between Fry and Norman. However, it’s hard to have good polling in such a race with no way to predict turnout for an unusual August special primary. Further, given the sprint of the race, there is not a ton of ability for candidates to make themselves known. There’s a sense that there will be geographic strengths—Fry will do well in his Myrtle Beach–based congressional district, Norman will do well in his home district around the Charlotte suburbs, and Sanford in his former congressional district in Charleston. But it is hard to predict. Whoever wins, it’ll be a coda to a long-standing push and pull between Graham, whose extensive Senate tenure saw him become a relic of a previous iteration of the GOP, and the populist right. The Reagan/Bush–era GOP that Graham came up in is now little better than a memory in Trump’s Washington. The result is that while Graham has been a larger-than-life figure in national politics, he was far more divisive among South Carolina Republicans. Even before his death, outside spending was required in Graham’s primary against his long-shot far-right opponent Mark Lynch in order to ensure that the incumbent got above the 50 percent mark needed to avoid a runoff. A pro-Graham super PAC spent millions of dollars attacking Lynch for having “a rap sheet fit for a convict, not a U.S. Senator.” Graham’s own campaign simply ran ads attacking Lynch for wanting to legalize cocaine. In contrast, Lynch pledged that if elected, he would pass legislation to force Turkey to make Istanbul’s Hagia Sophia a church again. Lynch insisted that it was Graham’s fault that the Hagia Sophia had become a mosque, seemingly confusing the South Carolina senator with Sultan Mehmed II, who ruled in what was then Constantinople after it was conquered on behalf of the Ottoman Turks in 1453.* With this opposition, Graham barely managed 56 percent of the vote. Lynch is running again in the special election but is not expected to be a major factor. In a mark of the race’s importance to the party’s future, donors are making their influence felt via super PACs. Darline Graham’s allied super PACs—as well as her campaign—have outspent her rivals. Of the nearly $4 million spent so far in the special primary on paid media, about $1.8 million has been spent by pro-Graham forces. Fry and his allies have spent just about $1 million and Sanford’s campaign has spent about $640,000. While the candidates themselves have run positive ads, their super PACs have gone negative. A pro–Darline Graham super PAC has run an ad depicting Fry as a piece of greasy meat being cooked by Trump and shaking hands with Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez. In contrast, Fry’s super PAC has attacked Norman as “a career politician who hired illegal immigrants.” It’s all about positioning for who can get into the runoff, and once they are there, anything can happen. But regardless of which combination emerges of the Trump-anointed cipher Darline Graham, the Freedom Caucus stalwart Norman, and the MAGA loyalist Fry, it will set the stakes for a dramatic runoff that will help determine the post-Trump future of the GOP. Correction, Aug. 11, 2026: This article originally misidentified Suleiman the Magnificent as the ruler of Constantinople in the 1450s. It was Sultan Mehmed II. New AI method reconstructs architectural lineages to guide heritage conservation
